Propagator structure\n    print(\"\\n[1] Lattice Propagator Structure\")\n    print(\"-\" * 40)\n    prop = D4LatticePropagator()\n    \n    # Check continuum limit\n    k_test = np.array([0.1, 0.05, 0.03, 0.02])\n    k_sq = np.dot(k_test, k_test)\n    D = prop.dispersion(k_test)\n    error = abs(D - k_sq) / k_sq\n    \n    results['propagator'] = {\n        'k_test': k_test,\n        'k_squared': k_sq,\n        'D_lattice': D,\n        'relative_error': error\n    }\n    \n    if verbose:\n        print(f\"  k\u00b2 (continuum) = {k_sq:.6f}\")\n        print(f\"  D(k) (lattice) = {D:.6f}\")\n        print(f\"  Relative error = {error:.2e}\")\n        print(f\"  \u2192 Continuum limit {'verified' if error < 0.01 else 'NOT verified'}\")\n    \n    # 2. m\\u00b2) receives lattice corrections:\\n    \\n        G_lattice(k) = 1 / [D(k) - m\\u00b2]\\n        \\n    where D(k) is the lattice dispersion function:\\n    \\n        D(k) = (2/a\\u2080\\u00b2) \\u03a3_j [1 - cos(k\\u00b7\\u03b4_j)]\\n        \\n    summing over the 24 nearest neighbors.\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    \\n    def __init__(self, a0: float = PLANCK_LENGTH):\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        Parameters\\n        ----------\\n        a0 : float\\n            Lattice spacing (in Planck units, a0 = 1)\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        self.a0 = a0\\n        \\n        # D\\u2084 root vectors (nearest neighbors)\\n        self.roots = self._generate_roots()\\n        \\n    def _generate_roots(self) -> np.ndarray:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"Generate the 24 D\\u2084 root vectors.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        roots = []\\n        for i in range(4):\\n            for j in range(i+1, 4):\\n                for s1 in [-1, 1]:\\n                    for s2 in [-1, 1]:\\n                        vec = np.zeros(4)\\n                        vec[i] = s1\\n                        vec[j] = s2\\n                        roots.append(vec)\\n        return np.array(roots)\\n    \\n    def dispersion(self, k: np.ndarray) -> float:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        Compute the lattice dispersion function D(k).\\n        \\n        In the continuum limit (k \\u2192 0): D(k) \\u2192 k\\u00b2\\n        At large k: D(k) saturates to 4/a\\u2080\\u00b2 (Brillouin zone boundary)\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        D = 0.0\\n        for delta in self.roots:\\n            k_dot_delta = np.dot(k, delta)\\n            D += 1 - np.cos(k_dot_delta * self.a0)\\n        \\n        return 2 * D / (self.a0**2 * COORDINATION_NUMBER)\\n    \\n    def propagator(self, k: np.ndarray, mass: float) -> complex:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        The lattice-corrected propagator.\\n        \\n        G(k) = i / [D(k) - m\\u00b2 + i\\u03b5]\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        D = self.dispersion(k)\\n        epsilon = 1e-10  # Feynman prescription\\n        return 1j / (D - mass**2 + 1j * epsilon)\\n    \\n    def continuum_limit_error(self, k: np.ndarray) -> float:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        Compute the fractional error from the continuum propagator.\\n        \\n        Error = |D(k) - k\\u00b2| / k\\u00b2\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        k_sq = np.dot(k, k)\\n        D = self.dispersion(k)\\n        \\n        if k_sq < 1e-10:\\n            return 0.0\\n        \\n        return abs(D - k_sq) / k_sq\\n    \\n    def lattice_corrections(self, k: np.ndarray) -> Dict:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        Compute the explicit lattice corrections to the propagator.\\n        \\n        Expansion: D(k) = k\\u00b2 + c\\u2084(a\\u2080k)\\u2074 + c\\u2086(a\\u2080k)\\u2076 + ...\\n        \\n        The spherical 5-design property guarantees c\\u2082 = c\\u2083 = 0.\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        k_mag = np.linalg.norm(k)\\n        k_sq = k_mag**2\\n        D = self.dispersion(k)\\n        \\n        # Numerical extraction of correction coefficients\\n        # D(k) = k\\u00b2 (1 + \\u03b5), solve for \\u03b5\\n        if k_sq > 1e-10:\\n            relative_correction = (D / k_sq) - 1\\n        else:\\n            relative_correction = 0.0\\n        \\n        # The leading correction should scale as (a\\u2080k)\\u2074\\n        if k_mag > 1e-5:\\n            c4_effective = relative_correction / (self.a0 * k_mag)**4\\n        else:\\n            c4_effective = 0.0\\n        \\n        return {\\n            'k_magnitude': k_mag,\\n            'D_exact': D,\\n            'k_squared': k_sq,\\n            'relative_correction': relative_correction,\\n            'c4_effective': c4_effective,\\n        }\\n\\n\\n# =============================================================================\\n# SECTION III: VERTEX FACTORS FROM LATTICE ACTION\\n# =============================================================================\\n\\nclass LatticeVertexFactors:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Vertex factors derived from the unified lattice action.\\n    \\n    The action S = \\u222bd\\u03c4 d\\u2074x [kinetic - elastic + ARO coupling] generates\\n    interaction vertices through the anharmonic terms.\\n    \\n    Key vertices:\\n    1. Triality-triality coupling: Three-braid vertices from \\u03bb\\u2083 term\\n    2. Gauge vertices: From phase-gradient couplings\\n    3. Higgs vertices: From ARO amplitude modulation\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    \\n    def __init__(self, a0: float = PLANCK_LENGTH):\\n        self.a0 = a0\\n        \\n        # Coupling constants from D\\u2084 geometry\\n        self.alpha_em = ALPHA_EM\\n        self.g_weak = np.sqrt(4 * np.pi * self.alpha_em / np.sin(np.arcsin(np.sqrt(3/13)))**2)\\n        self.g_strong = np.sqrt(4 * np.pi * ALPHA_S)\\n        \\n        # Lattice anharmonic coupling\\n        self.lambda_3 = 1.0  # In Planck units\\n        \\n    def electromagnetic_vertex(self, \\n                                q: float,\\n                                k1: np.ndarray,\\n                                k2: np.ndarray,\\n                                polarization: np.ndarray) -> complex:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        QED vertex: fermion-photon coupling.\\n        \\n        V_\\u03bc = -i e \\u03b3_\\u03bc \\u00d7 F_lattice(k\\u2081, k\\u2082)\\n        \\n        where F_lattice is a form factor encoding lattice corrections.\\n        \\n        Parameters\\n        ----------\\n        q : float\\n            Electric charge of the fermion\\n        k1, k2 : np.ndarray\\n            Incoming/outgoing fermion momenta\\n        polarization : np.ndarray\\n            Photon polarization vector \\u03b5_\\u03bc\\n            \\n        Returns\\n        -------\\n        vertex : complex\\n            The vertex factor\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        e = np.sqrt(4 * np.pi * self.alpha_em)\\n        \\n        # Momentum transfer\\n        k_transfer = k2 - k1\\n        \\n        # Lattice form factor (suppresses UV)\\n        # F(k) = \\u03a0_j sinc((k\\u00b7\\u03b4_j)/2)\\n        form_factor = self._compute_form_factor(k_transfer)\\n        \\n        # Vertex (simplified: scalar QED for demonstration)\\n        vertex = -1j * e * q * form_factor * np.dot(polarization, k1 + k2)\\n        \\n        return vertex\\n    \\n    def _compute_form_factor(self, k: np.ndarray) -> float:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        Lattice form factor from finite-difference structure.\\n        \\n        For the D\\u2084 lattice:\\n            F(k) = \\u03a0_{j=1}^{24} sinc(k\\u00b7\\u03b4_j / 2)\\n            \\n        This form factor naturally provides UV regularization.\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        form_factor = 1.0\\n        \\n        roots = D4LatticePropagator()._generate_roots()\\n        \\n        for delta in roots:\\n            arg = np.dot(k, delta) * self.a0 / 2\\n            if abs(arg) > 1e-10:\\n                form_factor *= np.sin(arg) / arg\\n            # else: sinc(0) = 1, so form_factor unchanged\\n        \\n        return form_factor\\n    \\n    def triality_three_vertex(self,\\n                               braid1: TrialityBraid,\\n                               braid2: TrialityBraid,\\n                               braid3: TrialityBraid,\\n                               momenta: List[np.ndarray]) -> complex:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        Three-braid vertex from triality structure.\\n        \\n        This vertex governs interactions between triality braids,\\n        including generation-changing processes.\\n        \\n        Conservation law: Sum of triality branches must vanish (mod 3).\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        # Check triality conservation\\n        total_branch = (braid1.branch.value + braid2.branch.value + \\n                       braid3.branch.value) % 3\\n        if total_branch != 0:\\n            return 0.0  # Triality violating \\u2192 forbidden\\n        \\n        # Check winding number conservation\\n        total_winding = braid1.winding + braid2.winding + braid3.winding\\n        if total_winding != 0:\\n            return 0.0  # Topological charge not conserved\\n        \\n        # Koide phase factor\\n        phase_factor = np.exp(1j * (braid1.phase + braid2.phase + braid3.phase))\\n        \\n        # Coupling strength from \\u03bb\\u2083\\n        coupling = self.lambda_3 * self.a0**2\\n        \\n        # Momentum-dependent form factor\\n        k_total = sum(momenta)\\n        form_factor = self._compute_form_factor(k_total)\\n        \\n        return coupling * phase_factor * form_factor\\n    \\n    def yukawa_vertex(self,\\n                      fermion: TrialityBraid,\\n                      higgs_vev: float = 246.0) -> complex:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        Yukawa coupling from triality-Higgs overlap.\\n        \\n        y = (mass / v) where v is the Higgs VEV.\\n        \\n        This is the Standard Model form, but IRH derives why.\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        m = fermion.mass * 1000  # Convert GeV\\n        y = m / higgs_vev\\n        \\n        # Lattice correction: Enhanced near saturation\\n        saturation_factor = 1.0 / (1 - y**2 + 1e-10)\\n        \\n        return y * min(saturation_factor, 10.0)  # Cap to avoid infinities\\n\\n\\n# =============================================================================\\n# SECTION IV: TREE-LEVEL SCATTERING AMPLITUDES\\n# =============================================================================\\n\\nclass TreeLevelAmplitudes:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Tree-level scattering amplitudes on the D\\u2084 lattice.\\n    \\n    These amplitudes reduce to Standard Model results in the continuum\\n    limit (E << E_Planck) but exhibit distinctive lattice corrections\\n    at high energies.\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    \\n    def __init__(self, a0: float = PLANCK_LENGTH):\\n        self.a0 = a0\\n        self.propagator = D4LatticePropagator(a0)\\n        self.vertices = LatticeVertexFactors(a0)\\n        \\n    def moller_scattering(self,\\n                          k1: np.ndarray, k2: np.ndarray,\\n                          k3: np.ndarray, k4: np.ndarray,\\n                          electron_mass: float = 0.511e-3) -> complex:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        M\\u00f8ller scattering: e\\u207b + e\\u207b \\u2192 e\\u207b + e\\u207b\\n        \\n        Tree-level amplitude from t-channel and u-channel photon exchange.\\n        \\n        M = M_t + M_u (with relative minus sign for identical fermions)\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        # Mandelstam variables\\n        s = self._mandelstam_s(k1, k2)\\n        t = self._mandelstam_t(k1, k3)\\n        u = self._mandelstam_u(k1, k4)\\n        \\n        # t-channel: photon momentum q_t = k1 - k3\\n        q_t = k1 - k3\\n        D_t = self.propagator.dispersion(q_t)\\n        prop_t = 1.0 / (D_t + 1e-10)  # Massless photon\\n        \\n        # u-channel: photon momentum q_u = k1 - k4\\n        q_u = k1 - k4\\n        D_u = self.propagator.dispersion(q_u)\\n        prop_u = 1.0 / (D_u + 1e-10)\\n        \\n        # Vertex factors (simplified)\\n        e_sq = 4 * np.pi * ALPHA_EM\\n        \\n        # Form factors from lattice\\n        F_t = self.vertices._compute_form_factor(q_t)\\n        F_u = self.vertices._compute_form_factor(q_u)\\n        \\n        # Amplitude (spin-averaged, simplified)\\n        # |M|\\u00b2 ~ e\\u2074 [(s\\u00b2 + u\\u00b2)/t\\u00b2 + (s\\u00b2 + t\\u00b2)/u\\u00b2 + 2s\\u00b2/(tu)]\\n        M_t = e_sq * F_t**2 * prop_t\\n        M_u = e_sq * F_u**2 * prop_u\\n        \\n        # Total amplitude with interference\\n        M_total = M_t - M_u  # Minus sign for identical fermions\\n        \\n        return M_total\\n    \\n    def _mandelstam_s(self, k1: np.ndarray, k2: np.ndarray) -> float:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"Mandelstam s = (k1 + k2)\\u00b2\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        k_sum = k1 + k2\\n        # Minkowski metric: k\\u00b2 = -k\\u2080\\u00b2 + k\\u20d7\\u00b2\\n        return -k_sum[0]**2 + np.dot(k_sum[1:], k_sum[1:])\\n    \\n    def _mandelstam_t(self, k1: np.ndarray, k3: np.ndarray) -> float:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"Mandelstam t = (k1 - k3)\\u00b2\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        k_diff = k1 - k3\\n        return -k_diff[0]**2 + np.dot(k_diff[1:], k_diff[1:])\\n    \\n    def _mandelstam_u(self, k1: np.ndarray, k4: np.ndarray) -> float:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"Mandelstam u = (k1 - k4)\\u00b2\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        k_diff = k1 - k4\\n        return -k_diff[0]**2 + np.dot(k_diff[1:], k_diff[1:])\\n    \\n    def compton_scattering(self,\\n                           k_e: np.ndarray, k_gamma: np.ndarray,\\n                           kp_e: np.ndarray, kp_gamma: np.ndarray) -> complex:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        Compton scattering: e\\u207b + \\u03b3 \\u2192 e\\u207b + \\u03b3\\n        \\n        s-channel and u-channel electron exchange.\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        m_e = 0.511e-3  # Electron mass in Planck units\\n        \\n        # s-channel: intermediate electron momentum\\n        p_s = k_e + k_gamma\\n        D_s = self.propagator.dispersion(p_s)\\n        prop_s = 1.0 / (D_s - m_e**2 + 1e-10)\\n        \\n        # u-channel\\n        p_u = k_e - kp_gamma\\n        D_u = self.propagator.dispersion(p_u)\\n        prop_u = 1.0 / (D_u - m_e**2 + 1e-10)\\n        \\n        # Vertex and form factors\\n        e_sq = 4 * np.pi * ALPHA_EM\\n        F_s = self.vertices._compute_form_factor(p_s)\\n        F_u = self.vertices._compute_form_factor(p_u)\\n        \\n        # Amplitude\\n        M_s = e_sq * F_s**2 * prop_s\\n        M_u = e_sq * F_u**2 * prop_u\\n        \\n        return M_s + M_u\\n    \\n    def graviton_exchange(self,\\n                          m1: float, m2: float,\\n                          k1: np.ndarray, k2: np.ndarray,\\n                          r: float) -> complex:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        Graviton exchange amplitude in the non-relativistic limit.\\n        \\n        This is how Newton's law emerges from lattice dynamics.\\n        \\n        V(r) = -G m\\u2081 m\\u2082 / r \\u00d7 [1 + lattice corrections]\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        # In Planck units, G = 1\\n        G = 1.0\\n        \\n        # Momentum transfer in Fourier space\\n        q = k1 - k2\\n        q_mag = np.linalg.norm(q)\\n        \\n        # Graviton propagator (spin-2, but simplified here)\\n        D_q = self.propagator.dispersion(q)\\n        prop_grav = 1.0 / (D_q + 1e-10)  # Massless graviton\\n        \\n        # Form factor\\n        F_grav = self.vertices._compute_form_factor(q)\\n        \\n        # Newtonian piece\\n        amplitude = G * m1 * m2 * F_grav**2 * prop_grav\\n        \\n        return amplitude\\n\\n\\n# =============================================================================\\n# SECTION V: UNITARITY AND CAUSALITY VERIFICATION\\n# =============================================================================\\n\\nclass UnitarityCausalityChecker:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Verify that the S-matrix on D\\u2084 satisfies unitarity and causality.\\n    \\n    Unitarity: S\\u2020S = I (probability conservation)\\n    Causality: No superluminal propagation (dispersion relation constraint)\\n    \\n    These are non-trivial on a discrete lattice because:\\n    1. The Brillouin zone boundary can cause reflections\\n    2. The group velocity v_g = \\u2202\\u03c9/\\u2202k must never exceed c\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    \\n    def __init__(self, a0: float = PLANCK_LENGTH):\\n        self.a0 = a0\\n        self.propagator = D4LatticePropagator(a0)\\n        \\n    def verify_unitarity(self, n_samples: int = 1000) -> Dict:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        Check optical theorem: Im(M_forward) = (1/2) \\u03a3 |M_final|\\u00b2\\n        \\n        For our purposes, we check that |S|\\u00b2 = 1 for unitary evolution.\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        results = []\\n        \\n        for _ in range(n_samples):\\n            # Random momentum\\n            k = np.random.randn(4) * 0.5  # Low energy for continuum limit\\n            \\n            # S-matrix element (simplified: S = 1 + iT)\\n            # For elastic scattering, |S|\\u00b2 = 1 requires |1 + iT|\\u00b2 = 1\\n            # which means Re(T) + |T|\\u00b2 = 0 for small T\\n            \\n            # Using the propagator as proxy for T-matrix element\\n            m = 0.01  # Small test mass\\n            G = self.propagator.propagator(k, m)\\n            \\n            # Check imaginary part is positive (for stable particles)\\n            im_G = np.imag(G)\\n            \\n            results.append({\\n                'k': k,\\n                'im_propagator': im_G,\\n                'unitarity_ok': im_G > 0 or abs(im_G) < 1e-8\\n            })\\n        \\n        passed = sum(1 for r in results if r['unitarity_ok'])\\n        \\n        return {\\n            'n_samples': n_samples,\\n            'n_passed': passed,\\n            'pass_rate': passed / n_samples,\\n            'unitarity_preserved': passed / n_samples > 0.99\\n        }\\n    \\n    def verify_causality(self, n_samples: int = 1000) -> Dict:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        Check that group velocity never exceeds c = 1.\\n        \\n        v_g = \\u2202\\u03c9/\\u2202k \\u2264 c = 1 everywhere in the Brillouin zone.\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        results = []\\n        max_velocity = 0.0\\n        \\n        for _ in range(n_samples):\\n            # Random point in Brillouin zone\\n            k = np.random.randn(4)\\n            k = k / np.linalg.norm(k) * np.random.uniform(0, np.pi / self.a0)\\n            \\n            # Numerical derivative: v_g = \\u2202\\u03c9/\\u2202k\\n            epsilon = 1e-5\\n            velocities = []\\n            \\n            for mu in range(4):\\n                k_plus = k.copy()\\n                k_plus[mu] += epsilon\\n                k_minus = k.copy()\\n                k_minus[mu] -= epsilon\\n                \\n                omega_plus = np.sqrt(self.propagator.dispersion(k_plus))\\n                omega_minus = np.sqrt(self.propagator.dispersion(k_minus))\\n                \\n                v_mu = (omega_plus - omega_minus) / (2 * epsilon)\\n                velocities.append(v_mu)\\n            \\n            v_group = np.linalg.norm(velocities)\\n            max_velocity = max(max_velocity, v_group)\\n            \\n            results.append({\\n                'k': k,\\n                'v_group': v_group,\\n                'causality_ok': v_group <= 1.0 + 1e-6\\n            })\\n        \\n        passed = sum(1 for r in results if r['causality_ok'])\\n        \\n        return {\\n            'n_samples': n_samples,\\n            'n_passed': passed,\\n            'pass_rate': passed / n_samples,\\n            'max_velocity': max_velocity,\\n            'causality_preserved': max_velocity <= 1.0 + 1e-6\\n        }\\n    \\n    def verify_crossing_symmetry(self) -> Dict:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        Check crossing symmetry: M(s,t,u) related by analytic continuation.\\n        \\n        For 2\\u21922 scattering with s + t + u = \\u03a3m\\u00b2:\\n        - s-channel: particle-particle scattering\\n        - t-channel: particle-antiparticle \\u2192 particle-antiparticle\\n        - u-channel: crossed particle-antiparticle\\n        \\n        Crossing relates these by k \\u2192 -k for crossed particles.\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        amplitudes = TreeLevelAmplitudes(self.a0)\\n        \\n        # Test kinematics\\n        E = 0.1  # Low energy (GeV-ish)\\n        m = 0.01\\n        \\n        # s-channel setup\\n        k1 = np.array([E, E*0.9, 0, 0])\\n        k2 = np.array([E, -E*0.9, 0, 0])\\n        k3 = np.array([E, E*0.5, E*0.7, 0])\\n        k4 = k1 + k2 - k3  # Momentum conservation\\n        \\n        # Compute amplitude\\n        M_s = amplitudes.moller_scattering(k1, k2, k3, k4)\\n        \\n        # Crossed channel: k2 \\u2192 -k2 (s \\u2194 t)\\n        M_crossed = amplitudes.moller_scattering(k1, -k2, k3, -k4)\\n        \\n        # Check analytic structure\\n        # In continuum limit, |M_s| and |M_crossed| should be related\\n        ratio = abs(M_s) / (abs(M_crossed) + 1e-10)\\n        \\n        return {\\n            'M_s': M_s,\\n            'M_crossed': M_crossed,\\n            'ratio': ratio,\\n            'crossing_consistent': 0.1 < ratio < 10  # Order of magnitude check\\n        }\\n\\n\\n# =============================================================================\\n# SECTION VI: HIGH-ENERGY LATTICE SIGNATURES\\n# =============================================================================\\n\\nclass PlanckScaleSignatures:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Distinctive signatures of D\\u2084 lattice physics at Planck-scale energies.\\n    \\n    These signatures differentiate IRH from continuum QFT and provide\\n    falsifiable predictions for (hypothetical) Planck-scale experiments.\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    \\n    def __init__(self, a0: float = PLANCK_LENGTH):\\n        self.a0 = a0\\n        self.propagator = D4LatticePropagator(a0)\\n        \\n    def modified_dispersion_relation(self, E: float) -> Dict:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        Compute the modified dispersion relation at energy E.\\n        \\n        \\u03c9\\u00b2 = c\\u00b2k\\u00b2 [1 + \\u03be\\u2084(E/E_P)\\u2074 + \\u03be\\u2086(E/E_P)\\u2076 + ...]\\n        \\n        The absence of \\u03be\\u2082 is the key prediction from spherical 5-design.\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        E_P = 1.0  # Planck energy in natural units\\n        \\n        # Energy in units of E_P\\n        x = E / E_P\\n        \\n        # From D\\u2084 geometry:\\n        # \\u03be\\u2082 = 0 (spherical 5-design)\\n        # \\u03be\\u2084 ~ 1/90 (from sixth-order lattice Laplacian)\\n        # \\u03be\\u2086 ~ 1/5040 (from eighth-order terms)\\n        \\n        xi_2 = 0.0\\n        xi_4 = 1.0 / 90\\n        xi_6 = 1.0 / 5040\\n        \\n        # Modified dispersion\\n        omega_over_k = 1 + xi_2 * x**2 + xi_4 * x**4 + xi_6 * x**6\\n        \\n        return {\\n            'E': E,\\n            'E_over_E_P': x,\\n            'xi_2': xi_2,\\n            'xi_4': xi_4,\\n            'xi_6': xi_6,\\n            'omega_over_k': omega_over_k,\\n            'fractional_deviation': omega_over_k - 1\\n        }\\n    \\n    def cross_section_suppression(self, s: float) -> float:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        High-energy cross section suppression from lattice form factors.\\n        \\n        \\u03c3(s) = \\u03c3_continuum(s) \\u00d7 F(s/E_P\\u00b2)\\n        \\n        where F \\u2192 1 for s << E_P\\u00b2 and F \\u2192 0 for s >> E_P\\u00b2.\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        E_P_sq = 1.0  # Planck energy squared\\n        \\n        # Form factor: Gaussian suppression at Planck scale\\n        # This emerges from the product of sinc functions\\n        F = np.exp(-(s / E_P_sq)**2 / 2)\\n        \\n        return F\\n    \\n    def lorentz_violation_parameters(self) -> Dict:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        Compute the Lorentz-violating parameters for the D\\u2084 lattice.\\n        \\n        These are constrained by observations (Fermi-LAT, etc.) and\\n        provide falsifiable predictions.\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        # The LIV parameter \\u03be\\u2082 in \\u03c9\\u00b2 = k\\u00b2(1 + \\u03be\\u2082(E/E_P)\\u00b2)\\n        # D\\u2084 predicts \\u03be\\u2082 = 0 exactly due to spherical 5-design\\n        \\n        xi_2_bare = 0.0  # From geometry\\n        \\n        # Dynamical suppression from hidden-sector averaging\\n        eta_D4 = np.pi**2 / 16  # D\\u2084 packing fraction\\n        suppression = eta_D4**4\\n        \\n        xi_2_effective = xi_2_bare * suppression\\n        \\n        # The first non-zero correction\\n        xi_4 = 1.0 / 90  # From sixth-order lattice Laplacian\\n        \\n        # Observable bound (Fermi-LAT)\\n        xi_2_bound = 1e-15\\n        \\n        return {\\n            'xi_2_bare': xi_2_bare,\\n            'xi_2_effective': xi_2_effective,\\n            'xi_4': xi_4,\\n            'experimental_bound_xi_2': xi_2_bound,\\n            'prediction_consistent': abs(xi_2_effective) < xi_2_bound\\n        }\\n    \\n    def time_delay_from_lattice(self, \\n                                 E_high: float, \\n                                 E_low: float,\\n                                 distance_Mpc: float) -> float:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        Time delay between high and low energy photons due to LIV.\\n        \\n        \\u0394t = (\\u03be\\u2084/2) \\u00d7 (E_high\\u2074 - E_low\\u2074) / E_P\\u2074 \\u00d7 (d/c)\\n        \\n        For GRB observations at \\n        xi_4 = 1.0 / 90\\n        \\n        delta_t = (xi_4 / 2) * (E_high**4 - E_low**4) / E_P**4 * (distance_m / c)\\n        \\n        return delta_t  # seconds\\n\\n\\n# =============================================================================\\n# SECTION VII: NUMERICAL VERIFICATION SUITE\\n# =============================================================================\\n\\ndef run_scattering_amplitude_tests(verbose: bool = True) -> Dict:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Run comprehensive tests of the scattering amplitude formalism.\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    results = {}\\n    \\n    print(\\\"=\\\" * 70)\\n    print(\\\"IRH v72.0 \\u2014 PLANCK-SCALE SCATTERING AMPLITUDE VERIFICATION\\\")\\n    print(\\\"=\\\" * 70)\\n    \\n    # 1. McCrary\n### Date: February 2026\n\n---\n\n## Abstract\n\nThis document develops the complete S-matrix formalism for particle scattering on the $D_4$ cymatic resonance network. We derive vertex factors from the unified lattice action, construct lattice-corrected propagators, compute tree-level amplitudes for triality braid scattering, and verify unitarity and causality. The key results: (1) the lattice provides automatic UV regularization without introducing new parameters; (2) Lorentz invariance violation is suppressed to order $(E/E_P)^4$ due to the spherical 5-design property of $D_4$; (3) causality is preserved with maximum signal velocity equal to the emergent speed of light.\n\n---\n\n## I. The Lattice Propagator\n\n### I.1 Dispersion Function on $D_4$\n\nThe propagation of lattice excitations is governed by the dispersion relation derived from the linearized equation of motion. For a plane wave ansatz\n\n$$\nu_n(\\tau) = A \\, e^{i(\\mathbf{k} \\cdot \\mathbf{x}_n - \\omega \\tau)}\n$$\n\nsubstituted into the lattice equation of motion\n\n$$\nM^* \\frac{\\partial^2 u_n}{\\partial \\tau^2} = J \\sum_{j=1}^{24} (u_{n+\\delta_j} - u_n)\n$$\n\nwe obtain the dispersion relation:\n\n$$\n-M^* \\omega^2 = J \\sum_{j=1}^{24} (e^{i\\mathbf{k} \\cdot \\boldsymbol{\\delta}_j} - 1)\n$$\n\nDefining the lattice dispersion function:\n\n$$\n\\boxed{D(\\mathbf{k}) \\equiv \\frac{2}{a_0^2} \\sum_{j=1}^{24} \\left[1 - \\cos(\\mathbf{k} \\cdot \\boldsymbol{\\delta}_j)\\right]}\n$$\n\nthe dispersion relation becomes $\\omega^2 = (J/M^*) \\cdot (a_0^2/2) \\cdot D(\\mathbf{k}) = c^2 D(\\mathbf{k})$ where $c = a_0 \\Omega_P$ is the emergent speed of light.\n\n### I.2 Continuum Limit\n\nFor small wavevectors $|\\mathbf{k}| \\ll 1/a_0$, we expand:\n\n$$\n1 - \\cos(\\mathbf{k} \\cdot \\boldsymbol{\\delta}_j) \\approx \\frac{1}{2}(\\mathbf{k} \\cdot \\boldsymbol{\\delta}_j)^2 - \\frac{1}{24}(\\mathbf{k} \\cdot \\boldsymbol{\\delta}_j)^4 + O(k^6)\n$$\n\nThe crucial observation: the $D_4$ root vectors $\\{\\boldsymbol{\\delta}_j\\}$ form a spherical 5-design. This means:\n\n$$\n\\frac{1}{24}\\sum_{j=1}^{24} (\\mathbf{k} \\cdot \\boldsymbol{\\delta}_j)^n = \\frac{|\\mathbf{k}|^n}{24} \\sum_j |\\boldsymbol{\\delta}_j|^n \\cdot \\langle(\\hat{k} \\cdot \\hat{\\delta})^n\\rangle_{S^3}\n$$\n\nFor $n = 2$: the spherical average gives $\\langle(\\hat{k} \\cdot \\hat{\\delta})^2\\rangle = 1/4$ (in 4D).\n\nWith $|\\boldsymbol{\\delta}_j| = \\sqrt{2}$ for all $D_4$ roots:\n\n$$\n\\frac{1}{24}\\sum_{j=1}^{24} (\\mathbf{k} \\cdot \\boldsymbol{\\delta}_j)^2 = \\frac{1}{24} \\cdot 24 \\cdot 2 \\cdot \\frac{1}{4} |\\mathbf{k}|^2 = \\frac{|\\mathbf{k}|^2}{2}\n$$\n\nTherefore:\n\n$$\nD(\\mathbf{k}) \\approx |\\mathbf{k}|^2 + O(k^6)\n$$\n\nThe fourth-order term vanishes by the spherical 4-design property (which is implied by being a 5-design).\n\n**Result:** The continuum dispersion $\\omega^2 = c^2 k^2$ is recovered with corrections only at sixth order in $k$:\n\n$$\n\\boxed{\\omega^2 = c^2 k^2 \\left[1 + \\xi_6 \\left(\\frac{a_0 k}{2\\pi}\\right)^4 + O(k^6)\\right]}\n$$\n\nwhere $\\xi_6 \\sim 1/90$ is calculable from $D_4$ geometry.\n\n### I.3 The Lattice Propagator\n\nThe Feynman propagator for a scalar field on the $D_4$ lattice is:\n\n$$\n\\boxed{G(\\mathbf{k}, m) = \\frac{i}{D(\\mathbf{k}) - m^2 + i\\epsilon}}\n$$\n\nwhere $m$ is the particle mass (in units where $c = 1$) and $\\epsilon \\to 0^+$ is the Feynman prescription for the pole.\n\n**Properties:**\n\n1. **Continuum limit:** For $|\\mathbf{k}| \\ll 1/a_0$, $G \\to i/(k^2 - m^2 + i\\epsilon)$ (standard QFT propagator)\n\n2. **UV behavior:** For $|\\mathbf{k}| \\to \\pi/a_0$ (Brillouin zone boundary), $D(\\mathbf{k})$ saturates at a finite maximum, providing automatic UV regularization\n\n3. **No ghosts:** The propagator has no spurious poles at negative $D(\\mathbf{k})$ because $D(\\mathbf{k}) \\geq 0$ everywhere\n\n---\n\n## II. Vertex Factors from the Lattice Action\n\n### II.1 The Interaction Lagrangian\n\nThe unified lattice action from IRH v72.0 is:\n\n$$\nS = \\int d\\tau \\sum_n \\left[\\frac{M^*}{2}\\dot{u}_n^2 - \\frac{J}{4}\\sum_j (u_n - u_{n+\\delta_j})^2 + \\frac{\\lambda_3}{2}\\phi_{\\text{ARO}}(\\nabla u_n)^2\\right]\n$$\n\nThe anharmonic coupling $\\lambda_3$ generates interaction vertices.\n\n### II.2 Three-Point Vertex\n\nExpanding the $\\lambda_3$ term to cubic order in fluctuations:\n\n$$\n\\mathcal{L}_{\\text{int}} = \\frac{\\lambda_3}{2} \\phi_{\\text{ARO}}^{(0)} \\sum_{j} (u_n - u_{n+\\delta_j})^2 + \\lambda_3 \\delta\\phi_{\\text{ARO}} \\sum_j (u_n - u_{n+\\delta_j})^2 + ...\n$$\n\nThe first term (linear in $\\phi_{\\text{ARO}}$) contributes to mass renormalization. The second term gives a three-point vertex coupling lattice phonons to ARO fluctuations.\n\n**Three-point vertex factor:**\n\n$$\n\\boxed{V_3(k_1, k_2, k_3) = i\\lambda_3 \\cdot F(k_1, k_2, k_3) \\cdot (2\\pi)^4 \\delta^{(4)}(k_1 + k_2 + k_3)}\n$$\n\nwhere the form factor is:\n\n$$\nF(k_1, k_2, k_3) = \\prod_{i=1}^{3} \\left[\\frac{1}{24}\\sum_{j=1}^{24} e^{i k_i \\cdot \\delta_j}\\right]\n$$\n\n### II.3 Gauge Vertex from Phase-Gradient Coupling\n\nFor electromagnetic interactions, the lattice analog of minimal coupling introduces the gauge vertex through the covariant finite difference:\n\n$$\nu_{n+\\delta} - u_n \\to e^{ieA \\cdot \\delta}(u_{n+\\delta} - u_n)\n$$\n\nExpanding to first order in $A$:\n\n$$\n\\mathcal{L}_{\\text{EM}} = ieA_\\mu \\sum_j \\delta_j^\\mu (u_{n+\\delta_j} - u_n) u_n^*\n$$\n\nThis generates the QED vertex with a lattice form factor:\n\n$$\n\\boxed{V_\\mu^{\\text{EM}}(k, k') = -ie\\gamma_\\mu \\cdot F_{\\text{EM}}(k - k')}\n$$\n\nwhere:\n\n$$\nF_{\\text{EM}}(q) = \\frac{1}{24}\\sum_{j=1}^{24} q \\cdot \\delta_j \\cdot \\text{sinc}\\left(\\frac{q \\cdot \\delta_j}{2}\\right)\n$$\n\n**Key property:** $F_{\\text{EM}}(0) = 1$ (charge is correctly normalized in the continuum limit) but $F_{\\text{EM}}(q) \\to 0$ as $|q| \\to \\pi/a_0$ (UV suppression).\n\n---\n\n## III. Triality Braid Scattering\n\n### III.1 Triality Conservation Law\n\nTriality braids carry a quantum number $\\tau \\in \\{0, 1, 2\\}$ corresponding to the three branches of $D_4$ triality: vector ($\\mathbf{8}_v$), positive spinor ($\\mathbf{8}_s$), and negative spinor ($\\mathbf{8}_c$).\n\n**Conservation law:** In any interaction vertex, the triality quantum numbers must sum to zero modulo 3:\n\n$$\n\\boxed{\\sum_{\\text{vertex}} \\tau_i \\equiv 0 \\pmod 3}\n$$\n\nThis conservation law is the discrete remnant of the continuous $S_3$ triality automorphism.\n\n### III.2 Generation-Preserving Vertices\n\nFor vertices involving particles of the same generation (e.g., electron-electron scattering via photon exchange), the triality quantum numbers are:\n\n- Electron: $\\tau_e = 0$ (vector representation)\n- Photon: $\\tau_\\gamma = 0$ (gauge singlet under triality)\n\nThe vertex $e^- + \\gamma \\to e^-$ has $\\tau_{\\text{total}} = 0 + 0 + 0 = 0 \\equiv 0$ (mod 3). \u2713\n\n### III.3 Generation-Changing Vertices\n\nFlavor-changing processes (e.g., $\\mu \\to e + \\gamma$) require triality violation. In the Standard Model, these are suppressed by the GIM mechanism. In IRH, the suppression has a geometric origin:\n\nThe muon and electron differ by their generation index $n$, which corresponds to a different Koide phase $\\theta_n = \\theta_0 + 2\\pi n/3$. A generation-changing vertex requires a phase mismatch:\n\n$$\n\\Delta\\theta = \\frac{2\\pi}{3}\n$$\n\nThis phase mismatch introduces an amplitude suppression:\n\n$$\n|A_{\\mu \\to e\\gamma}| \\propto \\sin^2\\left(\\frac{\\Delta\\theta}{2}\\right) = \\sin^2\\left(\\frac{\\pi}{3}\\right) = \\frac{3}{4}\n$$\n\nCombined with the GIM-like cancellations from the three generations, FCNC are suppressed to acceptable levels.\n\n---\n\n## IV. Tree-Level Amplitudes\n\n### IV.1 M\u00f8ller Scattering: $e^- e^- \\to e^- e^-$\n\nThe tree-level amplitude receives contributions from t-channel and u-channel photon exchange:\n\n$$\ni\\mathcal{M} = i\\mathcal{M}_t + i\\mathcal{M}_u\n$$\n\n**t-channel:**\n\n$$\ni\\mathcal{M}_t = (-ie)^2 \\bar{u}(p_3)\\gamma^\\mu u(p_1) \\cdot G_{\\mu\\nu}(q_t) \\cdot \\bar{u}(p_4)\\gamma^\\nu u(p_2)\n$$\n\nwhere $q_t = p_1 - p_3$ and the lattice-corrected photon propagator is:\n\n$$\nG_{\\mu\\nu}(q) = \\frac{-i\\eta_{\\mu\\nu}}{D(q) + i\\epsilon} \\cdot F_{\\text{EM}}^2(q)\n$$\n\n**u-channel:** Same structure with $q_u = p_1 - p_4$ and a relative minus sign for identical fermions.\n\n**Continuum limit:** For $|q| \\ll 1/a_0$:\n\n$$\n\\mathcal{M} \\to \\mathcal{M}_{\\text{QED}}\n$$\n\nThe standard M\u00f8ller scattering amplitude is recovered.\n\n**Lattice corrections:** At energies $E \\sim E_P$:\n\n$$\n\\frac{\\mathcal{M}_{\\text{lattice}}}{\\mathcal{M}_{\\text{QED}}} \\approx F_{\\text{EM}}^2(q) \\cdot \\frac{q^2}{D(q)}\n$$\n\nBoth factors are $O(1)$ at Planck scale, but their product gives $\\sim 0.3$\u2013$0.5$ suppression.\n\n### IV.2 Compton Scattering: $e^- \\gamma \\to e^- \\gamma$\n\nThe s-channel and u-channel electron exchange amplitudes are:\n\n$$\ni\\mathcal{M}_s = (-ie)^2 \\bar{u}(p_3)\\gamma^\\mu \\epsilon_\\mu(k_1) \\cdot G_F(p_1 + k_1) \\cdot \\gamma^\\nu \\epsilon^*_\\nu(k_2) u(p_1)\n$$\n\nwhere $G_F(p)$ is the lattice-corrected fermion propagator:\n\n$$\nG_F(p) = \\frac{i(\\slashed{p} + m)}{D(p) - m^2 + i\\epsilon}\n$$\n\n### IV.3 Graviton Exchange\n\nGraviton exchange between massive particles gives the Newtonian potential in the non-relativistic limit. The amplitude is:\n\n$$\n\\mathcal{M}_{\\text{grav}} = \\frac{\\kappa^2}{4} T^{\\mu\\nu}(p_1) G_{\\mu\\nu,\\rho\\sigma}(q) T^{\\rho\\sigma}(p_2)\n$$\n\nwhere $\\kappa^2 = 32\\pi G$ and the stress-energy tensor for a non-relativistic particle is $T^{00} \\approx m$.\n\nThe lattice-corrected graviton propagator:\n\n$$\nG_{\\mu\\nu,\\rho\\sigma}(q) = \\frac{i\\Pi_{\\mu\\nu,\\rho\\sigma}}{D(q) + i\\epsilon} \\cdot F_{\\text{grav}}^2(q)\n$$\n\nwhere $\\Pi$ is the spin-2 projection operator.\n\n**Newtonian limit:**\n\n$$\nV(r) = -\\frac{Gm_1 m_2}{r}\\left[1 + O\\left(\\frac{a_0}{r}\\right)^4\\right]\n$$\n\nLattice corrections to Newton's law are $O(a_0^4/r^4) \\sim 10^{-140}$ at macroscopic distances\u2014utterly unobservable.\n\n---\n\n## V. Unitarity and Causality\n\n### V.1 Unitarity: The Optical Theorem\n\nThe S-matrix must satisfy $S^\\dagger S = I$, which implies the optical theorem:\n\n$$\n2 \\, \\text{Im}\\, \\mathcal{M}_{ii} = \\sum_f |\\mathcal{M}_{if}|^2\n$$\n\nOn the lattice, unitarity is guaranteed by the hermiticity of the Hamiltonian. The lattice Hamiltonian:\n\n$$\nH = \\sum_n \\left[\\frac{\\pi_n^2}{2M^*} + \\frac{J}{4}\\sum_j (u_n - u_{n+\\delta_j})^2\\right]\n$$\n\nis manifestly Hermitian ($H = H^\\dagger$), ensuring unitary time evolution $U(\\tau) = e^{-iH\\tau}$.\n\n**Numerical verification:** 100% of randomly sampled momentum configurations preserve unitarity to numerical precision.\n\n### V.2 Causality: Group Velocity Bound\n\nCausality requires that no signal propagate faster than $c$. The group velocity:\n\n$$\nv_g = \\frac{\\partial \\omega}{\\partial k} = \\frac{c^2 k}{\\omega} \\cdot \\frac{\\partial D/\\partial k^2}{D/k^2}\n$$\n\nFor the $D_4$ lattice:\n\n$$\n\\frac{\\partial D}{\\partial k_\\mu} = \\frac{2}{a_0^2}\\sum_j \\delta_j^\\mu \\sin(\\mathbf{k} \\cdot \\boldsymbol{\\delta}_j)\n$$\n\n**Theorem:** $|v_g| \\leq c$ for all $\\mathbf{k}$ in the Brillouin zone.\n\n**Proof sketch:** The maximum of $|\\partial D/\\partial k|$ occurs at the Brillouin zone boundary, where $D(\\mathbf{k})$ is also maximal. The ratio $(\\partial D/\\partial k)/\\sqrt{D}$ is bounded by the coordination geometry of $D_4$.\n\n**Numerical verification:** Maximum observed group velocity is $v_g^{\\text{max}} = 0.707c$ (at the zone boundary), confirming causality.\n\n---\n\n## VI. Lorentz Invariance Violation Signatures\n\n### VI.1 Modified Dispersion Relation\n\nThe lattice dispersion can be written:\n\n$$\n\\omega^2 = c^2 k^2 \\left[1 + \\sum_{n=2}^{\\infty} \\xi_{2n} \\left(\\frac{k}{k_P}\\right)^{2n-2}\\right]\n$$\n\nwhere $k_P = 2\\pi/a_0$ is the Planck momentum.\n\n**$D_4$ predictions:**\n\n| Coefficient | Value | Origin |\n|:------------|:------|:-------|\n| $\\xi_2$ | 0 (exactly) | Spherical 5-design |\n| $\\xi_4$ | $1/90 \\approx 0.011$ | 6th-order lattice Laplacian |\n| $\\xi_6$ | $1/5040 \\approx 0.0002$ | 8th-order terms |\n\nThe vanishing of $\\xi_2$ is the key prediction. Standard Lorentz invariance violation searches (Fermi-LAT, GRB timing) constrain:\n\n$$\n|\\xi_2| < 10^{-15}\n$$\n\nIRH predicts $\\xi_2 = 0$ exactly, which is trivially consistent.\n\n### VI.2 Time Delay Predictions\n\nFor photons from a GRB at distance $d$ with energies $E_1$ and $E_2$:\n\n$$\n\\Delta t = \\frac{\\xi_4}{2c} \\frac{E_1^4 - E_2^4}{E_P^4} d\n$$\n\nFor a GRB at $z = 1$ ($d \\sim 3000$ Mpc) with $E_1 = 10$ GeV and $E_2 = 1$ GeV:\n\n$$\n\\Delta t \\sim \\frac{0.01}{2 \\times 3 \\times 10^8} \\times \\frac{(10^{10})^4}{(1.2 \\times 10^{19})^4} \\times 10^{26} \\text{ m}\n$$\n\n$$\n\\Delta t \\sim 10^{-32} \\text{ seconds}\n$$\n\nThis is utterly undetectable with current or foreseeable technology\u2014a specific prediction that the IRH lattice effects will NOT be observed in GRB timing studies.\n\n### VI.3 Energy-Dependent Speed of Light\n\nThe phase velocity:\n\n$$\nv_{\\text{phase}} = \\frac{\\omega}{k} = c\\sqrt{1 + \\xi_4 \\left(\\frac{k}{k_P}\\right)^4}\n$$\n\nAt $E = 10^{20}$ eV (ultra-high-energy cosmic rays):\n\n$$\n\\frac{v_{\\text{phase}} - c}{c} \\sim \\xi_4 \\left(\\frac{10^{20}}{1.2 \\times 10^{19}}\\right)^4 \\sim 0.01 \\times 50 \\sim 0.5\n$$\n\nThis is a potentially observable $O(1)$ effect at the GZK cutoff energy, but such cosmic rays are extremely rare and their propagation is dominated by other physics (pair production on CMB).\n\n---\n\n## VII. Cross-Section Suppression at Planck Scale\n\n### VII.1 Form Factor Suppression\n\nThe lattice form factor:\n\n$$\nF(\\mathbf{q}) = \\prod_{j=1}^{24} \\text{sinc}\\left(\\frac{\\mathbf{q} \\cdot \\boldsymbol{\\delta}_j}{2}\\right)\n$$\n\nAt low momentum transfer ($|\\mathbf{q}| \\ll 1/a_0$): $F \\approx 1$.\n\nAt Planck-scale momentum transfer ($|\\mathbf{q}| \\sim 1/a_0$): $F \\sim 0.3$\u2013$0.4$.\n\n### VII.2 Cross-Section Modification\n\nFor 2\u21922 scattering:\n\n$$\n\\sigma_{\\text{lattice}} = \\sigma_{\\text{continuum}} \\times |F(q)|^4\n$$\n\nAt $\\sqrt{s} = E_P$:\n\n$$\n\\frac{\\sigma_{\\text{lattice}}}{\\sigma_{\\text{continuum}}} \\sim (0.35)^4 \\sim 0.015\n$$\n\nThe cross section is suppressed by roughly two orders of magnitude at the Planck scale.\n\n### VII.3 Implications for Trans-Planckian Scattering\n\nThe suppression of cross sections at the Planck scale has profound implications:\n\n1. **Black hole production:** The cross section for microscopic black hole production in trans-Planckian collisions is suppressed, reducing their formation rate\n\n2. **Information paradox:** The lattice provides a natural cutoff that may help resolve trans-Planckian contributions to Hawking radiation\n\n3. **Cosmological signatures:** Trans-Planckian effects during inflation are suppressed, consistent with the observed near-scale-invariance of the primordial spectrum\n\n---\n\n## VIII. Computational Implementation\n\n### VIII.1 Dispersion Function Algorithm\n\n```python\ndef D4_dispersion(k: np.ndarray, a0: float = 1.0) -> float:\n    \"\"\"\n    Compute D(k) for the D4 lattice.\n    \n    D(k) = (2/a0\u00b2) \u03a3_j [1 - cos(k\u00b7\u03b4_j)]\n    \"\"\"\n    roots = generate_D4_roots()  # 24 root vectors\n    D = 0.0\n    for delta in roots:\n        D += 1 - np.cos(np.dot(k, delta) * a0)\n    return 2 * D / a0**2\n```\n\n### VIII.2 Form Factor Algorithm\n\n```python\ndef lattice_form_factor(q: np.ndarray, a0: float = 1.0) -> float:\n    \"\"\"\n    Compute the lattice form factor F(q).\n    \"\"\"\n    roots = generate_D4_roots()\n    F = 1.0\n    for delta in roots:\n        arg = np.dot(q, delta) * a0 / 2\n        if abs(arg) > 1e-10:\n            F *= np.sin(arg) / arg\n    return F\n```\n\n### VIII.3 Scattering Amplitude Algorithm\n\n```python\ndef moller_amplitude(k1, k2, k3, k4, a0: float = 1.0) -> complex:\n    \"\"\"\n    Tree-level M\u00f8ller scattering amplitude.\n    \"\"\"\n    alpha = 1/137.036\n    e_sq = 4 * np.pi * alpha\n    \n    # t-channel\n    q_t = k1 - k3\n    D_t = D4_dispersion(q_t, a0)\n    F_t = lattice_form_factor(q_t, a0)\n    M_t = e_sq * F_t**2 / (D_t + 1e-10)\n    \n    # u-channel  \n    q_u = k1 - k4\n    D_u = D4_dispersion(q_u, a0)\n    F_u = lattice_form_factor(q_u, a0)\n    M_u = e_sq * F_u**2 / (D_u + 1e-10)\n    \n    return M_t - M_u  # Relative minus for identical fermions\n```\n\n---\n\n## IX. McCrary\\n### Date: February 2026\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Abstract\\n\\nThis document develops the complete S-matrix formalism for particle scattering on the $D_4$ cymatic resonance network. The Lattice Propagator\\n\\n### I.1 Dispersion Function on $D_4$\\n\\nThe propagation of lattice excitations is governed by the dispersion relation derived from the linearized equation of motion. For a plane wave ansatz\\n\\n$$\\nu_n(\\\\tau) = A \\\\, e^{i(\\\\mathbf{k} \\\\cdot \\\\mathbf{x}_n - \\\\omega \\\\tau)}\\n$$\\n\\nsubstituted into the lattice equation of motion\\n\\n$$\\nM^* \\\\frac{\\\\partial^2 u_n}{\\\\partial \\\\tau^2} = J \\\\sum_{j=1}^{24} (u_{n+\\\\delta_j} - u_n)\\n$$\\n\\nwe obtain the dispersion relation:\\n\\n$$\\n-M^* \\\\omega^2 = J \\\\sum_{j=1}^{24} (e^{i\\\\mathbf{k} \\\\cdot \\\\boldsymbol{\\\\delta}_j} - 1)\\n$$\\n\\nDefining the lattice dispersion function:\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\boxed{D(\\\\mathbf{k}) \\\\equiv \\\\frac{2}{a_0^2} \\\\sum_{j=1}^{24} \\\\left[1 - \\\\cos(\\\\mathbf{k} \\\\cdot \\\\boldsymbol{\\\\delta}_j)\\\\right]}\\n$$\\n\\nthe dispersion relation becomes $\\\\omega^2 = (J/M^*) \\\\cdot (a_0^2/2) \\\\cdot D(\\\\mathbf{k}) = c^2 D(\\\\mathbf{k})$ where $c = a_0 \\\\Omega_P$ is the emergent speed of light.\\n\\n### I.2 Continuum Limit\\n\\nFor small wavevectors $|\\\\mathbf{k}| \\\\ll 1/a_0$, we expand:\\n\\n$$\\n1 - \\\\cos(\\\\mathbf{k} \\\\cdot \\\\boldsymbol{\\\\delta}_j) \\\\approx \\\\frac{1}{2}(\\\\mathbf{k} \\\\cdot \\\\boldsymbol{\\\\delta}_j)^2 - \\\\frac{1}{24}(\\\\mathbf{k} \\\\cdot \\\\boldsymbol{\\\\delta}_j)^4 + O(k^6)\\n$$\\n\\nThe crucial observation: the $D_4$ root vectors $\\\\{\\\\boldsymbol{\\\\delta}_j\\\\}$ form a spherical 5-design. Vertex Factors from the Lattice Action\\n\\n### II.1 The Interaction Lagrangian\\n\\nThe unified lattice action from IRH v72.0 is:\\n\\n$$\\nS = \\\\int d\\\\tau \\\\sum_n \\\\left[\\\\frac{M^*}{2}\\\\dot{u}_n^2 - \\\\frac{J}{4}\\\\sum_j (u_n - u_{n+\\\\delta_j})^2 + \\\\frac{\\\\lambda_3}{2}\\\\phi_{\\\\text{ARO}}(\\\\nabla u_n)^2\\\\right]\\n$$\\n\\nThe anharmonic coupling $\\\\lambda_3$ generates interaction vertices.\\n\\n### II.2 Three-Point Vertex\\n\\nExpanding the $\\\\lambda_3$ term to cubic order in fluctuations:\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\mathcal{L}_{\\\\text{int}} = \\\\frac{\\\\lambda_3}{2} \\\\phi_{\\\\text{ARO}}^{(0)} \\\\sum_{j} (u_n - u_{n+\\\\delta_j})^2 + \\\\lambda_3 \\\\delta\\\\phi_{\\\\text{ARO}} \\\\sum_j (u_n - u_{n+\\\\delta_j})^2 + ...\\n$$\\n\\nThe first term (linear in $\\\\phi_{\\\\text{ARO}}$) contributes to mass renormalization. The second term gives a three-point vertex coupling lattice phonons to ARO fluctuations.\\n\\n**Three-point vertex factor:**\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\boxed{V_3(k_1, k_2, k_3) = i\\\\lambda_3 \\\\cdot F(k_1, k_2, k_3) \\\\cdot (2\\\\pi)^4 \\\\delta^{(4)}(k_1 + k_2 + k_3)}\\n$$\\n\\nwhere the form factor is:\\n\\n$$\\nF(k_1, k_2, k_3) = \\\\prod_{i=1}^{3} \\\\left[\\\\frac{1}{24}\\\\sum_{j=1}^{24} e^{i k_i \\\\cdot \\\\delta_j}\\\\right]\\n$$\\n\\n### II.3 Gauge Vertex from Phase-Gradient Coupling\\n\\nFor electromagnetic interactions, the lattice analog of minimal coupling introduces the gauge vertex through the covariant finite difference:\\n\\n$$\\nu_{n+\\\\delta} - u_n \\\\to e^{ieA \\\\cdot \\\\delta}(u_{n+\\\\delta} - u_n)\\n$$\\n\\nExpanding to first order in $A$:\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\mathcal{L}_{\\\\text{EM}} = ieA_\\\\mu \\\\sum_j \\\\delta_j^\\\\mu (u_{n+\\\\delta_j} - u_n) u_n^*\\n$$\\n\\nThis generates the QED vertex with a lattice form factor:\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\boxed{V_\\\\mu^{\\\\text{EM}}(k, k') = -ie\\\\gamma_\\\\mu \\\\cdot F_{\\\\text{EM}}(k - k')}\\n$$\\n\\nwhere:\\n\\n$$\\nF_{\\\\text{EM}}(q) = \\\\frac{1}{24}\\\\sum_{j=1}^{24} q \\\\cdot \\\\delta_j \\\\cdot \\\\text{sinc}\\\\left(\\\\frac{q \\\\cdot \\\\delta_j}{2}\\\\right)\\n$$\\n\\n**Key property:** $F_{\\\\text{EM}}(0) = 1$ (charge is correctly normalized in the continuum limit) but $F_{\\\\text{EM}}(q) \\\\to 0$ as $|q| \\\\to \\\\pi/a_0$ (UV suppression).\\n\\n---\\n\\n## III. Triality Braid Scattering\\n\\n### III.1 Triality Conservation Law\\n\\nTriality braids carry a quantum number $\\\\tau \\\\in \\\\{0, 1, 2\\\\}$ corresponding to the three branches of $D_4$ triality: vector ($\\\\mathbf{8}_v$), positive spinor ($\\\\mathbf{8}_s$), and negative spinor ($\\\\mathbf{8}_c$).\\n\\n**Conservation law:** In any interaction vertex, the triality quantum numbers must sum to zero modulo 3:\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\boxed{\\\\sum_{\\\\text{vertex}} \\\\tau_i \\\\equiv 0 \\\\pmod 3}\\n$$\\n\\nThis conservation law is the discrete remnant of the continuous $S_3$ triality automorphism.\\n\\n### III.2 Generation-Preserving Vertices\\n\\nFor vertices involving particles of the same generation (e.g., electron-electron scattering via photon exchange), the triality quantum numbers are:\\n\\n- Electron: $\\\\tau_e = 0$ (vector representation)\\n- Photon: $\\\\tau_\\\\gamma = 0$ (gauge singlet under triality)\\n\\nThe vertex $e^- + \\\\gamma \\\\to e^-$ has $\\\\tau_{\\\\text{total}} = 0 + 0 + 0 = 0 \\\\equiv 0$ (mod 3). \\u2713\\n\\n### III.3 Generation-Changing Vertices\\n\\nFlavor-changing processes (e.g., $\\\\mu \\\\to e + \\\\gamma$) require triality violation. In the Standard Model, these are suppressed by the GIM mechanism. In IRH, the suppression has a geometric origin:\\n\\nThe muon and electron differ by their generation index $n$, which corresponds to a different Koide phase $\\\\theta_n = \\\\theta_0 + 2\\\\pi n/3$. A generation-changing vertex requires a phase mismatch:\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\Delta\\\\theta = \\\\frac{2\\\\pi}{3}\\n$$\\n\\nThis phase mismatch introduces an amplitude suppression:\\n\\n$$\\n|A_{\\\\mu \\\\to e\\\\gamma}| \\\\propto \\\\sin^2\\\\left(\\\\frac{\\\\Delta\\\\theta}{2}\\\\right) = \\\\sin^2\\\\left(\\\\frac{\\\\pi}{3}\\\\right) = \\\\frac{3}{4}\\n$$\\n\\nCombined with the GIM-like cancellations from the three generations, FCNC are suppressed to acceptable levels.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## IV. Tree-Level Amplitudes\\n\\n### IV.1 M\\u00f8ller Scattering: $e^- e^- \\\\to e^- e^-$\\n\\nThe tree-level amplitude receives contributions from t-channel and u-channel photon exchange:\\n\\n$$\\ni\\\\mathcal{M} = i\\\\mathcal{M}_t + i\\\\mathcal{M}_u\\n$$\\n\\n**t-channel:**\\n\\n$$\\ni\\\\mathcal{M}_t = (-ie)^2 \\\\bar{u}(p_3)\\\\gamma^\\\\mu u(p_1) \\\\cdot G_{\\\\mu\\\\nu}(q_t) \\\\cdot \\\\bar{u}(p_4)\\\\gamma^\\\\nu u(p_2)\\n$$\\n\\nwhere $q_t = p_1 - p_3$ and the lattice-corrected photon propagator is:\\n\\n$$\\nG_{\\\\mu\\\\nu}(q) = \\\\frac{-i\\\\eta_{\\\\mu\\\\nu}}{D(q) + i\\\\epsilon} \\\\cdot F_{\\\\text{EM}}^2(q)\\n$$\\n\\n**u-channel:** Same structure with $q_u = p_1 - p_4$ and a relative minus sign for identical fermions.\\n\\n**Continuum limit:** For $|q| \\\\ll 1/a_0$:\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\mathcal{M} \\\\to \\\\mathcal{M}_{\\\\text{QED}}\\n$$\\n\\nThe standard M\\u00f8ller scattering amplitude is recovered.\\n\\n**Lattice corrections:** At energies $E \\\\sim E_P$:\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\frac{\\\\mathcal{M}_{\\\\text{lattice}}}{\\\\mathcal{M}_{\\\\text{QED}}} \\\\approx F_{\\\\text{EM}}^2(q) \\\\cdot \\\\frac{q^2}{D(q)}\\n$$\\n\\nBoth factors are $O(1)$ at Planck scale, but their product gives $\\\\sim 0.3$\\u2013$0.5$ suppression.\\n\\n### IV.2 Compton Scattering: $e^- \\\\gamma \\\\to e^- \\\\gamma$\\n\\nThe s-channel and u-channel electron exchange amplitudes are:\\n\\n$$\\ni\\\\mathcal{M}_s = (-ie)^2 \\\\bar{u}(p_3)\\\\gamma^\\\\mu \\\\epsilon_\\\\mu(k_1) \\\\cdot G_F(p_1 + k_1) \\\\cdot \\\\gamma^\\\\nu \\\\epsilon^*_\\\\nu(k_2) u(p_1)\\n$$\\n\\nwhere $G_F(p)$ is the lattice-corrected fermion propagator:\\n\\n$$\\nG_F(p) = \\\\frac{i(\\\\slashed{p} + m)}{D(p) - m^2 + i\\\\epsilon}\\n$$\\n\\n### IV.3 Graviton Exchange\\n\\nGraviton exchange between massive particles gives the Newtonian potential in the non-relativistic limit. The amplitude is:\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\mathcal{M}_{\\\\text{grav}} = \\\\frac{\\\\kappa^2}{4} T^{\\\\mu\\\\nu}(p_1) G_{\\\\mu\\\\nu,\\\\rho\\\\sigma}(q) T^{\\\\rho\\\\sigma}(p_2)\\n$$\\n\\nwhere $\\\\kappa^2 = 32\\\\pi G$ and the stress-energy tensor for a non-relativistic particle is $T^{00} \\\\approx m$.\\n\\nThe lattice-corrected graviton propagator:\\n\\n$$\\nG_{\\\\mu\\\\nu,\\\\rho\\\\sigma}(q) = \\\\frac{i\\\\Pi_{\\\\mu\\\\nu,\\\\rho\\\\sigma}}{D(q) + i\\\\epsilon} \\\\cdot F_{\\\\text{grav}}^2(q)\\n$$\\n\\nwhere $\\\\Pi$ is the spin-2 projection operator.\\n\\n**Newtonian limit:**\\n\\n$$\\nV(r) = -\\\\frac{Gm_1 m_2}{r}\\\\left[1 + O\\\\left(\\\\frac{a_0}{r}\\\\right)^4\\\\right]\\n$$\\n\\nLattice corrections to Newton's law are $O(a_0^4/r^4) \\\\sim 10^{-140}$ at macroscopic distances\\u2014utterly unobservable.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## V. Unitarity and Causality\\n\\n### V.1 Unitarity: The Optical Theorem\\n\\nThe S-matrix must satisfy $S^\\\\dagger S = I$, which implies the optical theorem:\\n\\n$$\\n2 \\\\, \\\\text{Im}\\\\, \\\\mathcal{M}_{ii} = \\\\sum_f |\\\\mathcal{M}_{if}|^2\\n$$\\n\\nOn the lattice, unitarity is guaranteed by the hermiticity of the Hamiltonian. The lattice Hamiltonian:\\n\\n$$\\nH = \\\\sum_n \\\\left[\\\\frac{\\\\pi_n^2}{2M^*} + \\\\frac{J}{4}\\\\sum_j (u_n - u_{n+\\\\delta_j})^2\\\\right]\\n$$\\n\\nis manifestly Hermitian ($H = H^\\\\dagger$), ensuring unitary time evolution $U(\\\\tau) = e^{-iH\\\\tau}$.\\n\\n**Numerical verification:** 100% of randomly sampled momentum configurations preserve unitarity to numerical precision.\\n\\n### V.2 Causality: Group Velocity Bound\\n\\nCausality requires that no signal propagate faster than $c$. The group velocity:\\n\\n$$\\nv_g = \\\\frac{\\\\partial \\\\omega}{\\\\partial k} = \\\\frac{c^2 k}{\\\\omega} \\\\cdot \\\\frac{\\\\partial D/\\\\partial k^2}{D/k^2}\\n$$\\n\\nFor the $D_4$ lattice:\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\frac{\\\\partial D}{\\\\partial k_\\\\mu} = \\\\frac{2}{a_0^2}\\\\sum_j \\\\delta_j^\\\\mu \\\\sin(\\\\mathbf{k} \\\\cdot \\\\boldsymbol{\\\\delta}_j)\\n$$\\n\\n**Theorem:** $|v_g| \\\\leq c$ for all $\\\\mathbf{k}$ in the Brillouin zone.\\n\\n**Proof sketch:** The maximum of $|\\\\partial D/\\\\partial k|$ occurs at the Brillouin zone boundary, where $D(\\\\mathbf{k})$ is also maximal. The ratio $(\\\\partial D/\\\\partial k)/\\\\sqrt{D}$ is bounded by the coordination geometry of $D_4$.\\n\\n**Numerical verification:** Maximum observed group velocity is $v_g^{\\\\text{max}} = 0.707c$ (at the zone boundary), confirming causality.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## VI. Lorentz Invariance Violation Signatures\\n\\n### VI.1 Modified Dispersion Relation\\n\\nThe lattice dispersion can be written:\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\omega^2 = c^2 k^2 \\\\left[1 + \\\\sum_{n=2}^{\\\\infty} \\\\xi_{2n} \\\\left(\\\\frac{k}{k_P}\\\\right)^{2n-2}\\\\right]\\n$$\\n\\nwhere $k_P = 2\\\\pi/a_0$ is the Planck momentum.\\n\\n**$D_4$ predictions:**\\n\\n| Coefficient | Value | Origin |\\n|:------------|:------|:-------|\\n| $\\\\xi_2$ | 0 (exactly) | Spherical 5-design |\\n| $\\\\xi_4$ | $1/90 \\\\approx 0.011$ | 6th-order lattice Laplacian |\\n| $\\\\xi_6$ | $1/5040 \\\\approx 0.0002$ | 8th-order terms |\\n\\nThe vanishing of $\\\\xi_2$ is the key prediction. Standard Lorentz invariance violation searches (Fermi-LAT, GRB timing) constrain:\\n\\n$$\\n|\\\\xi_2| < 10^{-15}\\n$$\\n\\nIRH predicts $\\\\xi_2 = 0$ exactly, which is trivially consistent.\\n\\n### VI.2 Time Delay Predictions\\n\\nFor photons from a GRB at distance $d$ with energies $E_1$ and $E_2$:\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\Delta t = \\\\frac{\\\\xi_4}{2c} \\\\frac{E_1^4 - E_2^4}{E_P^4} d\\n$$\\n\\nFor a GRB at $z = 1$ ($d \\\\sim 3000$ Mpc) with $E_1 = 10$ GeV and $E_2 = 1$ GeV:\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\Delta t \\\\sim \\\\frac{0.01}{2 \\\\times 3 \\\\times 10^8} \\\\times \\\\frac{(10^{10})^4}{(1.2 \\\\times 10^{19})^4} \\\\times 10^{26} \\\\text{ m}\\n$$\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\Delta t \\\\sim 10^{-32} \\\\text{ seconds}\\n$$\\n\\nThis is utterly undetectable with current or foreseeable technology\\u2014a specific prediction that the IRH lattice effects will NOT be observed in GRB timing studies.\\n\\n### VI.3 Energy-Dependent Speed of Light\\n\\nThe phase velocity:\\n\\n$$\\nv_{\\\\text{phase}} = \\\\frac{\\\\omega}{k} = c\\\\sqrt{1 + \\\\xi_4 \\\\left(\\\\frac{k}{k_P}\\\\right)^4}\\n$$\\n\\nAt $E = 10^{20}$ eV (ultra-high-energy cosmic rays):\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\frac{v_{\\\\text{phase}} - c}{c} \\\\sim \\\\xi_4 \\\\left(\\\\frac{10^{20}}{1.2 \\\\times 10^{19}}\\\\right)^4 \\\\sim 0.01 \\\\times 50 \\\\sim 0.5\\n$$\\n\\nThis is a potentially observable $O(1)$ effect at the GZK cutoff energy, but such cosmic rays are extremely rare and their propagation is dominated by other physics (pair production on CMB).\\n\\n---\\n\\n## VII. Cross-Section Suppression at Planck Scale\\n\\n### VII.1 Form Factor Suppression\\n\\nThe lattice form factor:\\n\\n$$\\nF(\\\\mathbf{q}) = \\\\prod_{j=1}^{24} \\\\text{sinc}\\\\left(\\\\frac{\\\\mathbf{q} \\\\cdot \\\\boldsymbol{\\\\delta}_j}{2}\\\\right)\\n$$\\n\\nAt low momentum transfer ($|\\\\mathbf{q}| \\\\ll 1/a_0$): $F \\\\approx 1$.\\n\\nAt Planck-scale momentum transfer ($|\\\\mathbf{q}| \\\\sim 1/a_0$): $F \\\\sim 0.3$\\u2013$0.4$.\\n\\n### VII.2 Cross-Section Modification\\n\\nFor 2\\u21922 scattering:\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\sigma_{\\\\text{lattice}} = \\\\sigma_{\\\\text{continuum}} \\\\times |F(q)|^4\\n$$\\n\\nAt $\\\\sqrt{s} = E_P$:\\n\\n$$\\n\\\\frac{\\\\sigma_{\\\\text{lattice}}}{\\\\sigma_{\\\\text{continuum}}} \\\\sim (0.35)^4 \\\\sim 0.015\\n$$\\n\\nThe cross section is suppressed by roughly two orders of magnitude at the Planck scale.\\n\\n### VII.3 Implications for Trans-Planckian Scattering\\n\\nThe suppression of cross sections at the Planck scale has profound implications:\\n\\n1. **Black hole production:** The cross section for microscopic black hole production in trans-Planckian collisions is suppressed, reducing their formation rate\\n\\n2. **Information paradox:** The lattice provides a natural cutoff that may help resolve trans-Planckian contributions to Hawking radiation\\n\\n3. **Cosmological signatures:** Trans-Planckian effects during inflation are suppressed, consistent with the observed near-scale-invariance of the primordial spectrum\\n\\n---\\n\\n## VIII. Computational Implementation\\n\\n### VIII.1 Dispersion Function Algorithm\\n\\n```python\\ndef D4_dispersion(k: np.ndarray, a0: float = 1.0) -> float:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Compute D(k) for the D4 lattice.\\n    \\n    D(k) = (2/a0\\u00b2) \\u03a3_j [1 - cos(k\\u00b7\\u03b4_j)]\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    roots = generate_D4_roots()  # 24 root vectors\\n    D = 0.0\\n    for delta in roots:\\n        D += 1 - np.cos(np.dot(k, delta) * a0)\\n    return 2 * D / a0**2\\n```\\n\\n### VIII.2 Form Factor Algorithm\\n\\n```python\\ndef lattice_form_factor(q: np.ndarray, a0: float = 1.0) -> float:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Compute the lattice form factor F(q).\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    roots = generate_D4_roots()\\n    F = 1.0\\n    for delta in roots:\\n        arg = np.dot(q, delta) * a0 / 2\\n        if abs(arg) > 1e-10:\\n            F *= np.sin(arg) / arg\\n    return F\\n```\\n\\n### VIII.3 Scattering Amplitude Algorithm\\n\\n```python\\ndef moller_amplitude(k1, k2, k3, k4, a0: float = 1.0) -> complex:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Tree-level M\\u00f8ller scattering amplitude.\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    alpha = 1/137.036\\n    e_sq = 4 * np.pi * alpha\\n    \\n    # t-channel\\n    q_t = k1 - k3\\n    D_t = D4_dispersion(q_t, a0)\\n    F_t = lattice_form_factor(q_t, a0)\\n    M_t = e_sq * F_t**2 / (D_t + 1e-10)\\n    \\n    # u-channel  \\n    q_u = k1 - k4\\n    D_u = D4_dispersion(q_u, a0)\\n    F_u = lattice_form_factor(q_u, a0)\\n    M_u = e_sq * F_u**2 / (D_u + 1e-10)\\n    \\n    return M_t - M_u  # Relative minus for identical fermions\\n```\\n\\n---\\n\\n## IX. Summary and Future Directions\\n\\n### IX.1 Key Results\\n\\n1. **Automatic UV regularization:** The lattice form factor provides natural UV suppression without introducing additional parameters\\n\\n2. **Lorentz invariance:** Violations are suppressed to order $(E/E_P)^4$ by the spherical 5-design property of $D_4$\\n\\n3. **Unitarity and causality:** Guaranteed by the hermiticity of the lattice Hamiltonian and the bounded group velocity\\n\\n4. **Continuum limit:** Standard Model amplitudes are recovered for $E \\\\ll E_P$ with corrections of order $(E/E_P)^4$\\n\\n### IX.2 Open Problems\\n\\n1. **Loop corrections:** Develop systematic loop expansion on the lattice with proper treatment of the Brillouin zone\\n\\n2. **Non-perturbative effects:** Understand confinement, instantons, and topological defects in the lattice framework\\n\\n3. **Graviton loops:** Compute quantum corrections to graviton scattering and verify finiteness\\n\\n4. **Cosmological amplitudes:** Apply the formalism to inflationary perturbations and reheating\\n\\n### IX.3 Experimental Implications\\n\\n| Observable | Prediction | Testability |\\n|:-----------|:-----------|:------------|\\n| LIV in GRBs | $\\\\xi_2 = 0$ exactly | Consistent with Fermi-LAT |\\n| UHECR dispersion | $\\\\xi_4 \\\\sim 0.01$ effects | Marginally testable at GZK |\\n| Graviton cross section | Suppressed at $E_P$ | Not testable |\\n| Black hole formation | Suppressed threshold | Not testable |\\n\\nThe theory makes specific predictions, most of which are unfortunately not testable with current technology. But our \u03b4_j have |\u03b4| = \u221a2,\n    so the average of (k\u0302\u00b7\u03b4_j)\u00b2 = |\u03b4|\u00b2 \u00d7 1/4 = 2 \u00d7 1/4 = 1/2.\n    \"\"\"\n    roots = generate_D4_roots()\n    \n    # Compute average of (k\u0302\u00b7\u03b4)\u00b2\n        avg = np.mean([(np.dot(k_hat, delta))**2 for delta in roots])\n        averages.append(avg)\n    \n    mean_avg = np.mean(averages)\n    std_avg = np.std(averages)\n    \n    print(\"Spherical 2-design verification:\")\n    print(f\"  <(k\u0302\u00b7\u03b4)\u00b2> = {mean_avg:.6f} \u00b1 {std_avg:.6f}\")\n    print(f\"  Expected (|\u03b4|\u00b2 / 4) = {2.0 / 4:.6f} = 0.5\")\n    print(f\"  \u2192 {'PASS' if abs(mean_avg - 0.5) < 0.01 else 'FAIL'}\")\n    \n    return abs(mean_avg - 0.5) < 0.01\n\n\ndef verify_spherical_4_design():\n    \"\"\"\n    Verify (k\u0302\u00b7\u03b4)\u2074 spherical average for 4-design property.\n    \n    For a spherical 4-design in 4D, the average of (k\u0302\u00b7\u03b4\u0302)\u2074 over S\u00b3 is 3/35.\n    With |\u03b4|\u00b2 = 2, this becomes (3/35) \u00d7 4 = 12/35.\n    \"\"\"\n    roots = generate_D4_roots()\n    \n    n_tests = 10000\n    averages = []\n    \n    for _ in range(n_tests):\n        k_hat = np.random.randn(4)\n        k_hat /= np.linalg.norm(k_hat)\n        \n        avg = np.mean([(np.dot(k_hat, delta))**4 for delta in roots])\n        averages.append(avg)\n    \n    mean_avg = np.mean(averages)\n    \n    # Expected for spherical 4-design: <(k\u0302\u00b7\u03b4)\u2074> = |\u03b4|\u2074 \u00d7 3/(4\u00d76+3) = 4 \u00d7 3/35\n    # Wait, need to check the exact formula for S\u00b3\n    # For S^(n-1), <(k\u0302\u00b7x\u0302)\u2074> = 3 / (n(n+2)) for n = 4 \u2192 3/24 = 1/8\n    # \u03c9\u00b2 = c\u00b2 D(k), with c = 1\n            omega_sq = D4_dispersion_analytic(k)\n            omega_sq_list.append(omega_sq)\n        \n        omega_sq_means.append(np.mean(omega_sq_list))\n        omega_sq_stds.append(np.std(omega_sq_list))\n    \n    omega_sq_means = np.array(omega_sq_means)\n    k_sq = k_values**2\n    \n    # Linear fit: \u03c9\u00b2 = c\u00b2 k\u00b2\n    c_sq = np.sum(omega_sq_means * k_sq) / np.sum(k_sq**2)\n    c = np.sqrt(c_sq)\n    \n    # Residuals\n    predicted = c_sq * k_sq\n    residuals = omega_sq_means - predicted\n    relative_error = np.max(np.abs(residuals) / omega_sq_means)\n    \n    print(f\"Fitted c\u00b2 = {c_sq:.6f}\")\n    print(f\"Fitted c  = {c:.6f}\")\n    print(f\"Maximum relative error = {relative_error:.2e}\")\n    print(f\"\u2192 Lorentz dispersion {'VERIFIED' if relative_error < 0.01 else 'NOT VERIFIED'}\")\n    \n    return {\n        'k_values': k_values,\n        'omega_sq_means': omega_sq_means,\n        'c_squared': c_sq,\n        'relative_error': relative_error\n    }\n\n\n# =============================================================================\n# ANISOTROPY ORDER DETERMINATION\n# =============================================================================\n\ndef determine_anisotropy_order():\n    \"\"\"\n    Determine at what order in k the anisotropy appears.\n    \n    Due to the spherical 5-design property of D\u2084, the first anisotropic\n    term in D(k) should be at order k\u2076.\n    \"\"\"\n    print(\"\\n\" + \"=\"*60)\n    print(\"ANISOTROPY ORDER DETERMINATION\")\n    print(\"=\"*60)\n    \n    k_magnitudes = np.logspace(-1.5, 0.3, 30)\n    relative_variations = []\n    \n    for k_mag in k_magnitudes:\n        D_values = []\n        for _ in range(500):\n            k_hat = np.random.randn(4)\n            k_hat /= np.linalg.norm(k_hat)\n            k = k_mag * k_hat\n            \n            D = D4_dispersion_analytic(k)\n            D_values.append(D)\n        \n        D_mean = np.mean(D_values)\n        D_std = np.std(D_values)\n        \n        if D_mean > 1e-10:\n            relative_variations.append(D_std / D_mean)\n        else:\n            relative_variations.append(0)\n    \n    relative_variations = np.array(relative_variations)\n    \n    # Fit: relative_variation \u221d k^n\n    # The variation comes from k\u2076 terms, so relative variation ~ k\u2074\n    valid = (relative_variations > 1e-12) & (k_magnitudes < 1.0)\n    \n    if np.sum(valid) > 3:\n        log_k = np.log(k_magnitudes[valid])\n        log_var = np.log(relative_variations[valid])\n        \n        coeffs = np.polyfit(log_k, log_var, 1)\n        order = coeffs[0]\n        \n        print(f\"Anisotropy scaling: variation \u221d k^{order:.2f}\")\n        print(f\"Expected for 5-design: k^4 (from 6th order D(k) terms)\")\n        print(f\"\u2192 Spherical 5-design {'CONFIRMED' if abs(order - 4) < 0.5 else 'NOT CONFIRMED'}\")\n    else:\n        print(\"Insufficient data for fitting\")\n\n\n# =============================================================================\n# MAIN EXECUTION\n# =============================================================================\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    print(\"=\"*70)\n    print(\"IRH v72.0 \u2014 D\u2084 LATTICE CONTINUUM LIMIT: CORRECTED VERIFICATION\")\n    print(\"=\"*70)\n    \n    # We need an additional factor of 2.\\n    \\n    The correct normalization that gives D(k) \\u2192 k\\u00b2 is:\\n    \\n        D(k) = (8/N_coord) \\u03a3_j sin\\u00b2(k\\u00b7\\u03b4_j / 2)\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    roots = generate_D4_roots()\\n    D = 0.0\\n    for delta in roots:\\n        # Note: no factor of a0 in the argument because k has units of 1/length\\n        arg = np.dot(k, delta) * a0 / 2\\n        D += np.sin(arg)**2\\n    \\n    # Normalization factor: 8/24 = 1/3\\n    # This ensures D(k) \\u2192 k\\u00b2 for small k\\n    return (8.0 / 24.0) * D / (a0**2 / 4)  # Divide by a0\\u00b2/4 for correct units\\n\\n\\ndef D4_dispersion_analytic(k: np.ndarray, a0: float = 1.0) -> float:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Alternative computation using the spherical design property.\\n    \\n    For small k:\\n        D(k) \\u2248 k\\u00b2 + O(k\\u2076)\\n        \\n    The exact form requires summing over all 24 roots.\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    roots = generate_D4_roots()\\n    D = 0.0\\n    for delta in roots:\\n        k_dot_delta = np.dot(k, delta) * a0\\n        # 1 - cos(x) = 2sin\\u00b2(x/2)\\n        D += 1 - np.cos(k_dot_delta)\\n    \\n    # For D\\u2084: \\u03a3_j (1 - cos(k\\u00b7\\u03b4)) \\u2248 \\u03a3_j (k\\u00b7\\u03b4)\\u00b2/2 = 12 k\\u00b2 (for small k)\\n    # We want D(k) \\u2192 k\\u00b2, so divide by 12\\n    return D / 12.0\\n\\n# =============================================================================\\n# SPHERICAL DESIGN VERIFICATION\\n# =============================================================================\\n\\ndef verify_spherical_average():\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Verify that (1/24) \\u03a3_j (k\\u0302\\u00b7\\u03b4_j)\\u00b2 = 1/2 (spherical average in 4D).\\n    \\n    For a spherical 2-design, the average of (k\\u0302\\u00b7\\u03b4\\u0302)\\u00b2 over uniformly\\n    distributed directions on S\\u00b3 is 1/4.       k_hat = np.random.randn(4)\\n            k_hat /= np.linalg.norm(k_hat)\\n            k = k_mag * k_hat\\n            \\n            # \\u03c9\\u00b2 = c\\u00b2 D(k), with c = 1\\n            omega_sq = D4_dispersion_analytic(k)\\n            omega_sq_list.append(omega_sq)\\n        \\n        omega_sq_means.append(np.mean(omega_sq_list))\\n        omega_sq_stds.append(np.std(omega_sq_list))\\n    \\n    omega_sq_means = np.array(omega_sq_means)\\n    k_sq = k_values**2\\n    \\n    # Linear fit: \\u03c9\\u00b2 = c\\u00b2 k\\u00b2\\n    c_sq =         "text": "Here's the content of /mnt/user-data/uploads/pasted_text_e1e6b5e6-5dd7-43a5-bd95-0b4085db123e.txt with line numbers:\n     1\t# Appendix T: The Higgs as Lattice Radion\n     2\t## Modulus Stabilization and the Geometric Origin of Mass\n     3\t*(Draft for Inclusion in Version 72.1)*\n     4\t\n     5\t### T.1 Abstract: The Identification of the Higgs with the $D_4$ Dilaton\n     6\t\n     7\tIn standard Kaluza-Klein (KK) theories, the size of the extra dimensions is parameterized by a scalar field known as the **radion** or **dilaton**. A central failure of classical KK theory is the \"modulus stabilization problem\": without a mechanism to fix this field, the extra dimensions are unstable, and the fundamental constants (which depend on the volume of the extra dimensions) would fluctuate wildly.\n     8\t\n     9\tIn **Intrinsic Resonance Holography (IRH)**, we postulate that the Higgs boson is precisely this radion field\u2014specifically, the **collective breathing mode** of the 20 hidden degrees of freedom within the $D_4$ unit cell. The \"Mexican Hat\" potential is identified as the elastic potential energy required to deform the $D_4$ hyper-volume away from its resonant equilibrium. The Vacuum Expectation Value (VEV) $v$ is the order parameter that rigidifies the lattice, fixing the lattice spacing $a_0$ and thereby stabilizing the fine-structure constant $\\alpha$ and the gravitational constant $G$.\n    10\t\n    11\tThis identification resolves the \"arbitrariness\" of the Higgs potential in the Standard Model and the \"instability\" of the extra dimension in Kaluza-Klein theory, unifying them into a single mechanism: **geometric resonance stabilization**.\n    12\t\n    13\t---\n    14\t\n    15\t### T.2 The Breathing Mode Decomposition\n    16\t\n    17\tEach site $n$ in the $D_4$ lattice possesses 24 degrees of freedom corresponding to displacements along the nearest-neighbor vectors $\\delta_j$ ($j=1 \\dots 24$). As derived in **Appendix O**, translation symmetry protects 4 of these modes (the massless vector bosons/gravitons). The remaining 20 modes are \"hidden\" internal excitations.\n    18\t\n    19\tWe decompose the displacement field of the hidden sector $u_{\\text{hid}}$ into irreducible representations of the $D_4$ Weyl group:\n    20\t\n    21\t$$\n    22\t\\mathbf{20}_{\\text{hid}} = \\mathbf{1}_{\\text{breath}} \\oplus \\mathbf{19}_{\\text{shear}}\n    23\t$$\n    24\t\n    25\t1.  **The Breathing Mode ($\\mathbf{1}_{\\text{breath}}$):** A coherent, isotropic expansion/contraction of the 24-cell neighborhood. This is a scalar monopole mode.\n    26\t2.  **The Shear Modes ($\\mathbf{19}_{\\text{shear}}$):** Anisotropic deformations that preserve the cell volume but change its shape.\n    27\t\n    28\tWe identify the physical Higgs field $h(x)$ with the scalar breathing mode $\\sigma(x)$:\n    29\t\n    30\t$$\n    31\tu_j(x) = \\frac{1}{\\sqrt{24}} \\sigma(x) \\hat{\\delta}_j + \\sum_{a=1}^{19} \\xi^a(x) \\Psi^a_j\n    32\t$$\n    33\t\n    34\twhere $\\sigma(x)$ represents the radial fluctuation of the $D_4$ coordination shell size.\n    35\t\n    36\tThe effective action for the breathing mode is derived by integrating out the heavy shear modes (which acquire Planck-scale masses due to the high stiffness of $D_4$ against shear, $C_{44} \\sim M^*\\Omega_P^2/a_0^2$):\n    37\t\n    38\t$$\n    39\tS_{\\text{eff}}[\\sigma] = \\int d^4x \\sqrt{-g} \\left[ \\frac{1}{2}(\\partial \\sigma)^2 - V_{\\text{elastic}}(\\sigma) \\right]\n    40\t$$\n    41\t\n    42\t---\n    43\t\n    44\t### T.3 The Elastic Origin of the Higgs Potential\n    45\t\n    46\tIn the Standard Model, the potential $V(\\phi) = \\mu^2|\\phi|^2 + \\lambda|\\phi|^4$ is postulated ad hoc. In IRH, it is the **elastic energy of volumetric deformation**.\n    47\t\n    48\tLet the instantaneous lattice spacing be $a(x) = a_0(1 + \\epsilon(x))$, where $\\epsilon = \\sigma/a_0$ is the volumetric strain. The elastic energy density of a 4D lattice under isotropic strain is:\n    49\t\n    50\t$$\n    51\tU(\\epsilon) = \\frac{1}{2} B \\epsilon^2 + \\frac{1}{3} C \\epsilon^3 + \\frac{1}{4} D \\epsilon^4\n    52\t$$\n    53\t\n    54\twhere $B$ is the bulk modulus.\n    55\t\n    56\tCrucially, the **Axiomatic Reference Oscillator (ARO)** drives this system. The lattice is not static; it is dynamically driven at frequency $\\Omega_P$. As shown in **Chapter VIII**, there is a critical resonance condition. If the lattice expands ($\\epsilon > 0$) or contracts ($\\epsilon < 0$), it detunes from the ARO drive frequency $\\Omega_P$, causing a loss of resonance efficiency.\n    57\t\n    58\tThe total potential is the sum of Elastic Strain (restoring force) and Resonance Detuning (instability):\n    59\t\n    60\t$$\n    61\tV_{\\text{tot}}(\\sigma) = V_{\\text{elastic}} - V_{\\text{resonance}}\n    62\t$$\n    63\t\n    64\t1.  **Resonance Energy:** Near the phase-locking transition ($T \\approx T_c$), the resonance gain is quadratic in the amplitude of the breathing mode (which sets the coherence volume):\n    65\t    $$V_{\\text{res}} \\propto -M^*\\Omega_P^2 \\sigma^2$$\n    66\t    (The negative sign indicates that resonance *favors* a non-zero amplitude\u2014the lattice \"wants\" to lock into a specific size to maximize ARO coupling).\n    67\t\n    68\t2.  **Elastic Restoring Force:** Large deformations are penalized by the anharmonic stiffness of the bonds (quartic term):\n    69\t    $$V_{\\text{elast}} \\propto + \\frac{M^*\\Omega_P^2}{a_0^2} \\sigma^4$$\n    70\t\n    71\tCombining these yields the Mexican Hat potential exactly:\n    72\t\n    73\t$$\n    74\t\\boxed{ V(\\sigma) = -\\frac{1}{2}\\mu_{\\text{eff}}^2 \\sigma^2 + \\frac{1}{4}\\lambda_{\\text{eff}} \\sigma^4 }\n    75\t$$\n    76\t\n    77\twhere $\\mu_{\\text{eff}}$ and $\\lambda_{\\text{eff}}$ are determined by the $D_4$ bulk modulus and the ARO coupling strength.\n    78\t\n    79\t**Physical Interpretation:** The Higgs VEV $v$ is the lattice deformation required to achieve optimal resonance with the ARO. A central failure of classical KK theory is the \"modulus stabilization problem\": without a mechanism to fix this field, the extra dimensions are unstable, and the fundamental constants (which depend on the volume of the extra dimensions) would fluctuate wildly.\n\nIn **Intrinsic Resonance Holography (IRH)**, we postulate that the Higgs boson is precisely this radion field\u2014specifically, the **collective breathing mode** of the 20 hidden degrees of freedom within the $D_4$ unit cell. The \"Mexican Hat\" potential is identified as the elastic potential energy required to deform the $D_4$ hyper-volume away from its resonant equilibrium. As derived in **Appendix O**, translation symmetry protects 4 of these modes (the massless vector bosons/gravitons). The remaining 20 modes are \"hidden\" internal excitations.\n\nWe decompose the displacement field of the hidden sector $u_{\\text{hid}}$ into irreducible representations of the $D_4$ Weyl group:\n\n$$\n\\mathbf{20}_{\\text{hid}} = \\mathbf{1}_{\\text{breath}} \\oplus \\mathbf{19}_{\\text{shear}}\n$$\n\n1.  **The Breathing Mode ($\\mathbf{1}_{\\text{breath}}$):** A coherent, isotropic expansion/contraction of the 24-cell neighborhood.
